## Page 1


KOLMOGOROV EQUATIONS FOR STOCHASTIC VOLTERRA PROCESSES
WITH SINGULAR KERNELS
IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
Abstract. We associate backward and forward Kolmogorov equations to a class of fully nonlinear
Stochastic Volterra Equations (SVEs) with convolution kernels K that are singular at the origin.
Working on a carefully chosen Hilbert space H1, we rigorously establish a link between solutions of
SVEs and Markovian mild solutions of a Stochastic Partial Differential Equation (SPDE) of transport-
type. Then, we obtain two novel Itˆo formulae for functionals of mild solutions and, as a byproduct,
show that their laws solve corresponding Fokker–Planck equations. Finally, we introduce a natural
notion of “singular” directional derivatives along K and prove that (conditional) expectations of SVE
solutions can be expressed in terms of the unique solution to a backward Kolmogorov equation on H1.
Our analysis relies on stochastic calculus in Hilbert spaces, the reproducing kernel property of the
state space H1, as well as crucial invariance and smoothing properties that are specific to the SPDEs
of interest. In the special case of singular power-law kernels, our conditions guarantee well-posedness
of the backward equation either for all values of the Hurst parameter H, when the noise is additive,
or for all H > 1/4 when the noise is multiplicative.

1. Introduction
We leverage stochastic analysis on Hilbert spaces to extend the deep and fruitful connection between
Itˆo diffusions and Partial Differential Equations to the context of stochastic Volterra processes. The
latter are neither Markovian nor semimartingales and are given as solutions to Stochastic Volterra
Equations (SVEs) of the form
Xt = X0(t) +
Z t
0
K(t −s)b(Xs)ds +
Z t
0
K(t −s)σ(Xs) dWs,
t ≥0,
(1.1)
where W is a standard m−dimensional Wiener process with respect to a filtration (Ft)t≥0, b : Rd →
Rd, σ : Rd →Rd×m are measurable maps, K : R+ →Rd×d is a locally square integrable kernel and
X0 : R+ →Rd is an F0-measurable initial curve.
Via the choice of K, b, σ and X0, (1.1) includes many well known examples such as Mandelbrot–van
Ness (type I) and Riemann–Liouville (type II) fractional Brownian motions (fBms), rough volatil-
ity models [BFF+23], SDEs driven by additive fBm [Hai05], volatility modulated Volterra processes
[BNS08b], affine and polynomial Volterra processes [AJLP19, CT20, AJCP+24], fractional diffusions
[HLS10, LR20] (we refer to Section 1.5.1 for the specifications), while ambit fields describe an extension
with spatial interactions [Pod15, BNBV18]. Due to their ubiquity, Volterra processes are found in a
wide array of applications including volatility modeling [BFF+23], stochastic game theory [AJNV23],
electricity price modeling [BNBV13, Ben22], the study of turbulent flow velocities [BNS08a, Che17],
physical systems subject to non-Markovian forces [HLS10, CKK15], and climate science [EKN20]. In
turn, their importance in applications has sparked interest in more theoretical aspects such as well-
posedness in Banach spaces and connections with superprocesses [Zha10, MS15], (functional) limit
theorems and homogenization [BT15, DNN10, GLS22], stochastic integration [Dec05], extensions of
rough paths theory [HT21], functional inequalities [GJ23] and generalisations to anticipative coeffi-
cients [PP90, AN97] to name only a few.
Applications to rough volatility, which prompted considerable research in recent years, including
the present work, require the kernel to be singular at the origin i.e. limt↓0 |K(t)| = +∞. In terms
of the popular power-law kernel K(t) = tH−1
2 , which will be our guiding example in this paper, this
corresponds to the “rough” regime H ∈(0, 1
2). After closing the door of Markovianity behind us, we
are thus thrown out of the realm of semimartingales as well.
1.1. Goals and obstacles. In this new territory, located beyond the scope of many established
methods, we aim to provide a dynamical description of conditional expectations (E[ϕ(XT )|Ft])t∈[0,T ]
which, in rough volatility modelling, can be thought of as option prices with payoff ϕ and maturity T.
To this end, we develop forward and backward Kolmogorov equations, along with their well-posedness
theory, and characterise respectively the conditional laws and conditional expectations of Volterra
processes. These equations and the Itˆo formula leading to them lie at the core of the theory of Markov
processes and semimartingales, respectively.

KOLMOGOROV EQUATIONS FOR STOCHASTIC VOLTERRA PROCESSES WITH SINGULAR KERNELS

We reclaim Markovianity with the well-known idea of enlarging the state space of a non-Markovian
process to include (relevant information about) its past; we call this a lifting procedure. Since the works
of Carmona–Coutin [CC98] and Hairer [Hai05] this approach encountered many successes and allowed
to build upon results and intuition from Markov theory. The subsequent literature bears witness to
the diversity of available lifts and state spaces for each of them; we refer to Section 1.3 for an overview.
It also suggests that none of the proposed solutions subsumed their predecessors.
Notably, Viens
and Zhang [VZ19] derive a functional Itˆo formula for Volterra processes and exhibit the associated
Kolmogorov backward PDE. Despite its significance, well-posedness of the latter is only obtained in
the case of regular kernels [WYZ22, BR22] (H ≥1/2) or Gaussian processes [VZ19, BJP23, Pan23]
(b = 0, σ = 1 in (1.1)). For completeness we also mention the early work [Vyo05] for Gaussian processes
with regular kernels.
Generally speaking, a Markov process (λ(t))t≥0, evolving on a Polish space X, is called a lift
of (Xt)t≥0 if there exists a (continuous) projection operator π : X →Rd that recovers the Volterra pro-
cess π(λ(t)) = Xt. A natural idea, put forth in [AJEE19a, CT20], consists in introducing an additional
shift variable x ≥0 and defining
λ(t, x) := λ0(t + x) +
Z t
0
K(t −s + x)b(Xs)ds +
Z t
0
K(t −s + x)σ(Xs)dWs.
It is clear that we should have Xt = λ(t, 0) and, since Xt+x = λ(t, x) + X⊥(t, x) where X⊥(t, x)
is independent from Ft, we also expect that (λ(t, x))x∈[0,T −t] is a “path-dependent” state variable
which adequately captures the history of X. This parametrisation is reminiscent of the Heath–Jarrow–
Morton–Musiela (HJMM) [HJM92, Mus93] framework for forward rates in mathematical finance. Be-
sides, λ is linked (at least when b is linear) to the so-called forward variance curves (E[Xt+x|Ft])x≥0.
For these reasons we call λ the curve lift of X.
In order to study λ independently of X we shall write an equation for its dynamics. Informally we
observe that λ ought to satisfy the stochastic evolution equation
λ(t) = S(t)λ0 +

the point evaluation map ev0 at x = 0 plays the role of the projection π introduced above.
The
lift property is then satisfied as ev0(λ(t)) = Xt. In turn, our attention may shift from X to the
Markovian (yet infinite dimensional) λ since properties of the former can be obtained from the latter
after evaluating at x = 0. Specifically, λ embeds all the relevant path-dependent information of X
since, for all sufficiently regular test functions ϕ, there exists some measurable function u that satisfies
E[ϕ(XT )|Ft] = u(t, λ(t)).
(1.3)

space X of our lift; this is however a non-trivial affair. To make matters worse, the singularity of the
kernel leads to a cascade of three puzzles, and hence the three objectives of this work.

1.2. Contributions. To the best of our knowledge, the present work is the first to address all three
of these objectives in the setting of fully non-linear SVEs with singular kernels. Our main results are
summarised below (complete versions of the theorems can be found in subsequent sections):
A) The state space. We identify a weighted Sobolev space X ≡H1 := H1
w(R+; Rd) as a natural
state space for λ. H1 is a separable Hilbert space and the shift semigroup S has the particular property
of regularising the kernel since K(t + ·) is continous for any t > 0. Working on this space we obtain
mild well-posedness of (1.4), when b, σ are Lipschitz continuous and grow at most linearly, even though
the coefficients do not belong to H1
w :
Theorem A. (See Theorem 3.7). The SPDE (1.4) has a unique H1−valued mild solution.
The weights w that we use, and will later be called admissible (Definition 2.2), are chosen to
compensate for singularities of K both at the origin and at infinity. In addition, they must satisfy
certain technical regularity conditions ensuring that K and its derivatives are square integrable when
shifted away from the origin. In the special case of the power-law kernel K(t) = tH−1
2 , H ∈(0, 1/2),
we prove that w(x) = xβe−x, for β ∈(1 −2H, 1), provides just the right amount of regularity around
zero (for more details we refer the reader to Example 2.11 below).
Riding on this success, the flow, Markov, Feller and generalised Feller properties quickly follow. We
further obtain, even without semimartingality, a mild Itˆo formula for λ which relies on recent work
of Da Prato, Jentzen and R¨ockner [DPJR19]. The latter gives rise to an Itˆo–Volterra formula for X
itself, as well as a Fokker–Planck (or forward Kolmogorov) equation, see Sections 4.4.1,4.4.2.
B) A singular Itˆo formula. Unfortunately, the mild Itˆo formula does not lead to the derivation of
a backward PDE and, as alluded to earlier, H1−valued solutions of (1.4) can only be defined in a mild,
non-semimartingale form (recall that K, ∂xλ(t) /∈H1). Once again, we leverage the crucial smoothing
property of shifts acting on K, this time to identify an invariant subspace K1 ⊂H1 for the paths
of λ. The space K1 (3.18) is defined as a subspace of functions y such that y, ∂xy are “smoothened”
when shifted by any positive t > 0. Indeed it turns out that, if, for all δ > 0, S(δ)∂xλ(t) ∈H1
at t = 0 then the same holds for all t > 0. Restricting initial conditions to K1, we are able to obtain
a “semimartingale-type” Itˆo formula for certain functionals of mild solutions λ (1.2) which reads as
follows:

Again, an important observation here is that differentials DF(y)(K), D2F(y)(K, K) must be taken
along directions K that are not elements of H1 or K1. For this reason, we define the latter as limits
of classical Gateaux derivatives DF(y)(S(δ)K), D2F(y)(S(δ)K, S(δ)K), as δ →0, and name them
singular directional derivatives. This notion is both natural and necessary to obtain this “singular Itˆo
formula”; a precise definition of singular derivatives, along a class of admissible directions K , and
several examples are provided in Section 4.3. Indeed our proof strategy relies on the application of a
standard Hilbert space Itˆo formula to the semimartingale (S(δ)λ(t))t≥0 and then a limiting argument
as δ →0. Finally, Theorem B. leads directly to a singular (non-mild) Fokker–Planck equation (4.29)
and plays a major role in our subsequent analysis of the backward PDE.
C) The singular Kolmogorov backward PDE. In virtue of the singular Itˆo formula, we obtain
a Feynman–Kac formula and thus unique probabilistic representations of solutions to a novel singular
Kolmogorov backward PDE on H1. The challenging part consists in proving that u is in fact twice
“singularly” differentiable and that u, Du, D2u are sufficiently regular. This requires a fine analysis of
the derivatives of λ, with respect to initial data, and their convergence properties as S(δ)K tends to K.
A complete study of the resulting variation equations and corresponding tangent processes cannot be
found elsewhere in the literature. On a slightly technical note, our work here is facilitated once again
by the special structure of (1.4) since the nonlinear coefficients b, σ only depend on finite-dimensional
projections of the solution. Thus, in sharp contrast to SPDEs with Nemytskii (or composition) oper-
ators, the nonlinearities here are in fact Fr´echet differentiable; see also Remark 5.4 below for a more
detailed discussion. More importantly, unless σ is constant, our proof highlights that K must be in
Lq([0, T]; Rd×d) with q > 4. This threshold seems to be structural and not an artefact of our method,
as we explain in Remark 5.9. For the power-law kernel K(t) = tH−1
2 , this corresponds to H > 1/4.2

1.3. Relevant literature. Our proposed lift, especially written in the strong formulation (1.4), is
closely related to the HJMM model for forward rates in mathematical finance [HJM92, Mus93], albeit
with singular coefficients. Several theoretical results have been established for this Musiela SPDE;
these include e.g.
well-posedness with different types of noises [Fil01, FT03, Mar10], Kolmogorov
equations [GM01], invariant measures [Teh05, CT06] to name only a few.
In particular, Filipovic
identifies a class of weighted RKHS as natural state spaces for the solution [Fil01, Chapter 5]. The
2It is worth noting that this “1/4” condition has appeared in the completely different context of geometric rough

IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
conditions he imposes on the weight are financially-driven but unfit for our purposes. These spaces
are proposed by Cuchiero and Teichmann [CT19] as a state space for Markovian lifts of affine Volterra
processes. There, the authors define weakly mild solutions to the equation and derive an exponential
affine formula which is essentially a backward PDE with a particular type of test function.
Simultaneously, Abi Jaber and El Euch [AJEE19a] introduce the curve lift to discuss the Markov
and invariant properties (in R × R+) of the Volterra Heston model. The emphasis is on determining
the geometry of the state space to guarantee non-negativity of the variance process.
If one does not wish to exploit the SPDE structure of the curve lift then it is also possible to work
without the Musiela parametrisation and define the lift ˜λ(t, T) = λ(t, T −t). From an option pricing
viewpoint, this shifts the focus from time to maturity T −t to time of maturity T. In this context we
can mention [BF23] where the authors derive, in the additive noise case, the existence of a backward
PDE in an L2(R+) space directly from a Taylor expansion. If one enforces the assumption K(0) < +∞
then most of the obstacles outlined above disappear and more tools become available, opening the gates
to stochastic control [PT24]. As discussed above, in the closest paper to ours, Viens and Zhang [VZ19]
prove a functional Itˆo formula for ˜λ(t). Instead of a superficial comparison, we provide in Section 6.2
a comprehensive review of their results and of the developments offered by the present paper.
The other most popular lift in the literature, initiated by Carmona and Coutin in [CC98] for
fBms and extended to SVEs by Harms and Stefanovits [HS19], is the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) lift.
The latter can be embedded in Lp(µ) spaces with different measures/weights µ [Ham24], spaces of
measures [CT19] or negative Sobolev spaces [Hub24].
Notably, this approach led to advances in
stochastic control for Volterra processes [AJMP21, Ham25] but its wide recognition comes (in our
humble opinion) from the Markovian multifactor approximation it entails [CC98, AJEE19b].
The
lifting method adopted by Hamaguchi in particular [Ham24] seems to have similar features as ours.
We demonstrate in Section 6.1 how one can pass from the curve lift to the OU lift and vice-versa.
1.4. Perspectives. Having resolved (under certain conditions) the three problems outlined above, we
believe this paper presents a framework that is not an end in itself but a base camp from which we
can meet further objectives. For the first time, we have access to a complete and rigorous, Hilbert
space based theory that delivers Itˆo formula and Kolmogorov PDEs for Volterra processes. These can
be used as tools in applications such as stochastic Taylor expansions and cubature formulae [FZ23],
weak error rates analysis [BJP23], support characterisation [Kal21] and non-linear filtering [CP24].
Numerically solving the backward PDE is interesting to the mathematical finance and rough volatility
communities where conditional expectations correspond to option prices, see for instance [PS24] and
Section 1.5.2.
A higher summit that sparks a lot of interest is the study of controlled Volterra processes. With
the foundations laid out here, we will tackle Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equations and their viscosity
solutions in the future. Such an expedition has only ever been attempted with the regular kernel
oxygen bottle.
The two Fokker–Planck equations, mild and singular, presented in this paper are a central tool
for the analysis of invariant measures and (a non-linear version thereof) shall characterise the law of
mean-field SVEs as it does in the semimartingale case [CD22].
On the foundational level, theoretical work is still required to relax the assumptions made in this
paper and to extend the theory to the multiplicative noise case with H ≤1/4 (in the spirit of [BD18]),
to non-smooth test functions φ (checking whether the Markov semigroup regularises the test function),
to path-dependent test functions and to non-convolution kernels. We believe the latter extension not
to require radically new ideas: the change of parametrisation K(t, s)σ(x) = ˜σ(t, s, x) = ˆσ(t −s, s, x)
allows to develop similar arguments as in this paper at the expense of working with time-dependent
coefficients since S(t −s)Kσ ◦ev0(λ) shall be replaced by S(t −s)ˆσ(·, s, ev0(λ)) in (1.2).
In mathematical finance, rough volatility and forward variance models are natural fields of applica-
tions but other markets with term structure models may benefit from this forward curve formalism,
such as foreign exchange or energy markets [FPT24]. We refer to Section 1.5.2 for a short overview of
option pricing in these different contexts.

specifications as above. Of course, our results which rely on Markovianity of the lift (i.e. well-
posedness and derivation of the backward Kolmogorov PDE) apply to the state-dependent
case σt = σ(Xt) for some sufficiently well-behaved σ.

1.5.2. Option pricing applications. As one can infer from the last example, a number of asset price
models in mathematical finance are accurately represented by SVEs such as (1.1). In turn, as alluded to
in the introduction, options prices are conditional expectations of a certain payoff function, represented
here by φ(λ(T)). In this context, such prices are thus characterised by the backward PDE (1.5).
We highlight below that, for a variety of underlying assets of interest, the relevant payoff functions
do not only depend on XT but on the forward curve (E[XT +x|Ft])x∈[a,b], for some interval [a, b] ⊂R+.
We thus start by showcasing the situations in which λ can be represented as the conditional expectation
λ(t, x) = E[Xt+x|Ft], t, x ≥0, making our choice of lift particulary suited for the pricing task at hand.
Let b(y) = ay, a ∈Rd×d, in (1.1) and Ra be the resolvent of the second kind of aK, defined as the
solution of aK −Ra = aK ⋆Ra where ⋆denotes the convolution (this resolvent exists and is unique
under Assumption 2.6). A variation of constants formula of Volterra type [GLS90, Chapter 2, Theorem
3.5] yields the expression

We highlight four model specifications below. The first three are displayed under a risk-neutral

• Rough volatility. In the rough volatility model described in the last example of Section 1.5.1,
Xt = (log(St/S0), Vt), t ≥0 is a model for the joint log-price and volatility. For an option
of payoff ϕ ∈C2

derived in [BJP23] in the setting of the rough Bergomi model where V is a Gaussian process.
This paper makes the leap to fully non-linear Volterra dynamics.

1.6. Outline. The rest of this paper is organised as follows:
In Section 2 we fix all the necessary notation and introduce our standing assumptions and function
spaces that will be used throughout this work.
We also define a class of admissible weights Wa
(Definition 2.2) and corresponding scales of weighted Sobolev spaces H, Hm, m ∈N which we use for
the well-posedness of (1.4). Furthermore, we elaborate on the validity of our assumptions for the
particular yet central case of power-law kernels (Example 2.11).
Section 3 is devoted to the analysis of the SPDE 1.4. Properties of the shift semigroup {S(t)}t≥0
on H1, as well as existence and uniqueness of H1−valued mild solutions (Theorem 3.7) are proved
in Subsection 3.1. Then, we prove lift and temporal regularity properties of mild solutions λ (1.2) in
Subsection 3.2. In Subsection 3.3 we show that the subspace K1 ⊂H1, as described in Subsection 1.2,
is invariant for the SPDE dynamics. Flow and regularity properties of λ, as well as (generalized)


## Page 10


10
IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
Feller properties of the corresponding Markov semigroup, are then obtained in Subsections 3.4, 3.5
respectively.
With the basic properties of the underlying Markovian dynamics at hand, we set out to prove several
Itˆo formulae for functionals of the state process λ in Section 4. First, we remark that analytically strong
SPDE solutions (and consequently a strong Itˆo formula) are only available on the larger Hilbert space
H ⊃H1. Since the latter is not an RKHS, the resulting strong Itˆo formula (Proposition 4.4, Subsection
4.1) cannot be projected to Rd via evaluation and thus is not useful for the study of the SVE (1.1). After
these preliminary observations, we invoke results of [DPJR19] to obtain a mild Itˆo formula (Theorem
4.8) for smooth functions of λ in Subsection 4.2. Even though the latter does not lead to a well-posed
backward Kolmogorov PDE, it does lead to an Itˆo formula for (1.1) as well as a mild Fokker–Planck
(or forward) equation for the laws {µt}t≥0 ⊂P(H1) of λ. These applications are presented in the first
two parts of Subsection 4.4. Finally, Subsection 4.3 contains our results on the singular Itˆo formula
(Theorem 4.18) which is the backbone of the singular Kolmogorov PDE. Here, we define the concept
of singular directional derivatives (Definition 4.15) along a set of directions K ⊂H (3.17) and present
several examples and non-examples that shed light on this notion of smoothness. Then, we introduce a
class C1,2
T,K (Definition 4.15) of singularly differentiable functionals and proceed to the proof of Theorem
4.18 for functionals of this class. As mentioned above, the latter leads to a singular Fokker–Planck
equation for the laws {µt}t≥0 ⊂P(H1), when tested against C1,2
T,K functions. The latter is presented
as a third and last application in the last part of Section 4.4. Our mild and singular Itˆo formulae
apply to different classes of test functions F and lead to Volterra and semimartingale representations
of F(λ), respectively. That being said, in this work we only provide a complete well-posedness theory
for the corresponding singular Kolmogorov equations. A more detailed comparison between the two
can be found in Remark 4.22.
Section 5 is entirely devoted to the study of the backward Kolmogorov equation (1.5). The main
result of this paper, Theorem 5.1, is stated in the beginning of this section and asserts that a strong
(pointwise) solution of (1.5) is given by the Markov semigroup of λ. The same theorem shows that
this probabilistic solution is unique among the class of C1,2
T,K functions. The characterisation (1.3) for
conditional expectations of X is proved in the subsequent Corollary 5.2. The proof of Theorem 5.1 is
postponed to Subsection 5.4 and is preceded by several directional differentiability results of (1.2) with
respect to initial data in H1. In particular: We obtain well-posedness of extended (in the sense that
the directions lie in K ) first and second variation equations in Subsection 5.1; see Lemmas 5.5 and
5.8. For “regular” directions h, h′ ∈H1, the corresponding mild solutions ζh, ζh,h′ coincide with the
Gateaux derivatives Dyλy(h), D2
yλy(h, h′) (per Corollary 5.12). In Subsection 5.2 we show that the map
y 7−→λy is “singularly” differentiable in the direction of K /∈H1 and identify the singular differentials
Dλy(K), D2λy(K, K) with the processes ζK, ζK,K′. Finally, Subsection 5.3 contains several auxiliary
convergence results for “mollified” variants of λ, ζh, ζh,h′. To be slightly more precise, we substitute
various instances of the singular kernel K (or, more generally, a singular direction h ∈K ), appearing
either as coefficient of equations or as initial condition of the tangent process ζK, with the shifted
smooth kernels Kδ = S(δ)K, δ > 0, and show that the corresponding processes converge as δ →0 to
their well-defined “singular” counterparts. These results are necessary for the proof of Theorem 5.1
which requires two approximation procedures by smooth functions (in the level of both initial data
and directions).
As we have already argued, we believe that the SPDE framework put forth in this paper is both
natural and fruitful for the study of SVEs with singular kernels. Its effectiveness in the derivation
of Kolmogorov PDEs for SVEs is clearly showcased by our main results.
Indeed, the use of shift
semigroups (or transport SPDEs) properly encodes the “smoothing” of singular kernels away from
the origin. Most of the results and constructions stemming from the developed theory are essentially
manifestations of this phenomenon. That being said, there are (at least) two other popular and success-
ful choices of Markovian lifts for SVEs in the relevant literature: the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) lift of
Carmona–Coutin [CC98] and the Path-Dependent PDEs (PPDEs) introduced by Viens–Zhang [VZ19].

11
In Section 6 we provide a detailed comparison of the curve lift and our results to those of the afore-
mentioned papers. In Subsection 6.1 we show that the OU lift can be obtained, via Laplace transform,
by an invertible transformation of the SPDE (1.4). Ultimately, we discuss PPDEs in more detail in
Subsection 6.2.
A few auxiliary results related to properties of the state space H1 and the proof of a Volterra–
Gr¨onwall inequality are gathered in Appendix A.

Definition 2.8. Let H be a Hilbert space of functions f : R+ →Rd. H is called a Reproducing Kernel
Hilbert Space (RKHS) if, for all x ∈R+, the evaluation operator
H ∋f 7−→evx(f) := f(x) ∈Rd

3. Well-posedness and properties of the SPDE
This section is devoted to the study of the transport-type system of SPDEs
(
∂tλ(t, x) = ∂xλ(t, x) + Kb(λ(t, 0)) + Kσ(λ(t, 0)) ˙Wt

duce some relevant tools from semigroup theory.
3.1. Shift semigroup on weighted Sobolev spaces and mild solutions. For t ≥0, f ∈L1
loc(R+)
we introduce the left-shift map
S(t)f(x) := f(x + t), x ∈R+.
(3.2)
To be slightly more precise, S(t) can first be defined for f ∈C(M) for any compact M ⊂R+ and then
uniquely extended by density to L1(M).
Lemma 3.1. If w ∈Wa, the following hold:
(1) Let t ≥0, Ct := sups≥t

(2) The family {S(t)}t≥0 ⊂L (H) defines a strongly continuous semigroup.
(3) The derivative operator ∂x : Dom(∂x) ⊂H →H generates S on H and Dom(∂x), endowed
with the graph norm, coincides with H1 as a Banach space.
The same conclusions hold with the pair (H, H1) replaced by (Hm, Hm+1) for some m ∈N.
We postopone the proof to Appendix A.2.
Remark 3.2. From a glance at the proof of Lemma 3.1 we see that the shift semigroup is strongly
continuous when viewed as a family of operators in L (H1). Indeed for any f ∈H1, S(t)f is weakly
differentiable with ∂xS(t)f = S(t)f ′. Moreover, since f ′ ∈H, strong continuity of S implies that
|S(t)f ′|H ≤Ct|f ′|H for a constant Ct > 0. Clearly, the same conclusions hold with H1 replaced by Hm
and f ′ replaced by higher order derivatives.
Corollary 3.3. There exist c, C > 0 such that for all t ≥0, f ∈H1
|S(t)f|H ≤Cect|f|H.
In particular, the constant Ct introduced in Lemma 3.1(i) satisfies Ct ≤Cect. The same conclusion
(albeit with possibly different constants) holds if H is replaced by H1.
Proof. Both conclusions follow directly from the Hille-Yosida theorem (see e.g.
[DPZ14, Theorem
A.3]).

c (0, ∞) ⊂

Remark 3.5. We stress here that C∞
c (0, ∞) is not dense in the spaces Hm for m ∈N but it is dense
as a subset of H. Indeed, if the former was true, then any f ∈Hm would have a unique continuous
representative with ev0f = f(0) = 0. Since we do not want to prescribe fixed values at the origin, we
also avoid defining Hm as closures of smooth, compactly supported test functions.

for all {Ft}t≥0-stopping times τ.
The strong Feller property holds if the Markov semigroup regularises the test function, in the sense
that Ptφ ∈Cb(H1) for all bounded measurable φ. Hamaguchi [Ham24, Section 3.2] demonstrates,
for a different Markovian lift but in a similar setup as ours where the Brownian motion W is finite-
dimensional, that such a property cannot hold. This is due to the degeneracy of the noise for SPDEs


## Page 26


26
IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
such as (3.19). We can remedy to this issue with a detour to generalised Feller processes. In the finite-
dimensional theory, two definitions of Feller semigroups coexist. One is related to the space of continu-
ous bounded functions Cb(H1) and another one based on the space of continuous functions vanishing at
infinity C0(H1). The former generalises to the infinite-dimensional case in a straightforward manner, as
we just presented, but is seldom strongly continuous. The latter formulation, however, needs revamp-
ing when working with infinite dimensions. Generalized Feller processes [DT10, CT20, CMT23] were
introduced with the purpose of establishing a Feller theory in non-locally compact (typically infinite-
dimensional) spaces, with the immediate benefit of granting strong continuity to the semigroup. The
semigroups act on weighted spaces analogous to the space C0 in non-locally compact spaces.
We
take [CT20] as reference to recall the notations.
We first note that H1 is a completely regular Hausdorff topological space. We say that ϱ: H1 →
(0, +∞) is an admissible weight function if the sublevel sets KR := {x ∈H1 : ϱ(x) ≤R} are compact
for all R > 0 [CT20, Definition 2.1]. The vector space
Bϱ(H1) :=

φ: H1 →R : sup
x∈H1
ϱ(x)−1 |φ(x)| < ∞

,
equipped with the norm
|φ|ϱ := sup
x∈H1
ϱ(x)−1 |φ(x)| ,
(3.22)
is a Banach space, and Cb(H1) ⊂Bϱ(H1).
Moreover, the space Bϱ(H1) is defined as the closure
of Cb(H1) with respect to |·|ϱ [CT20, Definition 2.3], and is also a Banach space when equipped with
the norm (3.22). A useful characterisation, proven in [CMT23, Theorem 2.2], states that φ ∈Bϱ(H1)
if and only if, for all R > 0, φ|KR∈Cb(KR) and
lim
R→∞
sup
x∈H1\KR
|φ(x)|
ϱ(x) = 0.
(3.23)
Generalised Feller semigroups consist of bounded, positive, linear, bounded operators and are auto-
matically strongly continuous.
Definition 3.19 (Definition 2.5 of [CT20]). A family of bounded linear operator Pt : Bϱ(H1) →
Bϱ(H1) for t ≥0 is called a generalised Feller semigroup if
i) P0 = I, the identity on Bϱ(H1),
ii) Pt+s = PtPs, for all t, s ≥0,
iii) For all φ ∈Bϱ(H1) and y ∈H1, limt↓0 Ptφ(y) = φ(y),
iv) There exist C > 0 and ε > 0 such that for all t ∈[0, ε], ∥Pt∥L (Bϱ(H1)) ≤C,
v) Pt is positive for all t ≥0, that is, for any φ ∈Bϱ(H1) such that φ ≥0, then Ptφ ≥0.
Crucially, generalized Feller semigroups are strongly continuous by [CMT23, Theorem 2.8]. More-
over, this theory found applications to the study of the large-time behaviour of Volterra process [FK24,
JPS25].
One can prove that a time-homogeneous Markov process has the generalized Feller property using
the conditions displayed in [CT20, Theorem 2.21]. The strategy consists in embedding a closed sub-
space E into a dual space in order to leverage the weak-⋆-topology. Since H1 is a Hilbert space, we
simply define E = H⋆
1 = H1, equipped with its weak topology. The Banach-Alaoglu theorem ensures
that ϱ(x) = 1+|x|H1 is an admissible weight function because closed balls with respect to the H1-norm
are compact in the weak topology.
Proposition 3.20. Let Assumptions 2.4 i)-ii) and 2.6 i)-ii) hold. The time-homogeneous Markov
semigroup (Pt)t≥0 satisfies the following:
(1) There are constants C > 0 and ε > 0 such that
Ptϱ(y) ≤Cϱ(y),
for all y ∈H1, t ∈[0, ε].
(2) It holds that
lim
t→0 Ptφ(y) = φ(y),
for all y ∈H1, φ ∈Bϱ(H1).

4. Itˆo formulae and Fokker–Planck equations
Up until this point we have connected the strong solution X of (1.1) to (probabilistically strong)
analytically mild solutions λ of the SPDE (3.1) that take values on H1. This section is devoted to the
proof of Itˆo formulae for λ which yield, as byproducts, an Itˆo formula for X as well as Fokker-Planck
equations for the law of λ.
As we have already mentioned above, our framework allows for singular kernels K that are not
necessarily elements of H1. This means in particular, that the mild solutions λ (3.4) cannot be viewed
as H1−valued semimartingales of the form
λ(t, x) = λ0(x) +
Z t

Indeed, notice that such a representation cannot make sense on H1 (or any other RKHS for that
matter): evaluating the solution at x = 0 and using the lift property (3.15) would result to the
contradicting statement Xt = ∞for all t. In the first part of this section, we show that such expressions
can only be justified by viewing λ as a process that takes values on the larger Hilbert space H.
The absence of analytically strong solutions means that classical infinite-dimensional Itˆo formulae
are of little to no use for mild solutions. Viewing λ as a semimartingale in H (which is not an RKHS)
prevents us from obtaining any useful information for the Volterra process X. In the second part of
this section we show that mild Itˆo formulae, similar to the ones developed in [DPJR19], provide a
viable way to circumvent such problems. Last but not least, we derive a “singular” Itˆo formula which
combines the best of both approaches—semimartingale formulation and RKHS.

Remark 4.3. It is important to observe the differences between the transport SPDEs (4.2) and (3.4).
The former is cast as an evolution on H with random coefficients while the latter is posed as an
evolution equation on the RKHS H1 with (non-random) Lipschitz nonlinearities. More importantly,
solutions of the former do not a priori satisfy the Markov property due to the presence of random
coefficients. In sharp contrast, solutions of (3.1) are Markovian as shown in Proposition 3.16. The
point here is that a mild solution of (3.1), starting from “smooth” initial conditions λ0 ∈H1, can be
viewed as a strong solution of (4.2) that takes values on the larger space H.
The point of view we adopt here allows us to obtain a “strong” Itˆo formula for the unique mild
solution of (3.1) when viewed as a process with values on H.

Remark 4.5. We emphasise once again that strong Itˆo formula (4.4) holds with respect to the Hilbert
space H and not H1. Despite its simplicity, H does not enjoy the RKHS property and hence such
an Itˆo formula is not suitable to obtain information about smooth functionals of the SVE solution
X· = ev0(λ(·)). This is due to the fact that the evaluation functional ev0 is not continuous and a
fortiori not differentiable when defined on H.

The class of mild Itˆo process is tailor-made to include mild solutions of a large class of SPDEs.

Mild Itˆo processes are clearly not semimartingales and are not expected to satisfy a classical Itˆo
formula.
Fortunately, a mild Itˆo formula was developed in [DPJR19] (see also [Hub24, VS24] for
applications of mild Itˆo formulae; as well as [GSS23, Lemma 5.4] for a similar type of Itˆo formula in
the context of slow-fast systems of SPDEs). The following is the main result of this section and shows
that a mild Itˆo formula also holds for mild solutions of (3.1) despite the irregularity of coefficients.

Remark 4.9 (Comparison with the path-dependent Itˆo formula from [VZ19]). In the setting of SVEs
with convolution-type kernels, the mild Itˆo formula in Theorem 4.8 is significantly more tractable
compared to the path-dependent Itˆo formula from [VZ19, Theorems 3.10, 3.17]. Indeed, the differentials
are all interpreted in the sense of classical Fr´echet differentiation on Hilbert spaces and the mild Itˆo
formula holds both in the case of singular and non-singular kernels without additional assumptions on
the coefficients. We refer to Section 6.2 for a comprehensive comparison.
4.3. Singular directional differentiation and singular Itˆo formula. We have showed that H−
valued strong solutions of the transport-type SPDE (3.1) satisfy a strong Itˆo formula (Proposition 4.4)
while the H1−valued mild solutions (3.1) satisfy the mild Itˆo formula from Theorem 4.8. As discussed
in Remark 4.5, the former cannot be applied to functionals of the Volterra process X = ev0(λ) and H
is not an RKHS. As for the latter, even though it leads to an Itˆo formula for functionals of X and a
Fokker–Planck equation (Section 4.4.2), it cannot be directly applied to obtain a backward Kolmogorov


## Page 33


KOLMOGOROV EQUATIONS FOR STOCHASTIC VOLTERRA PROCESSES WITH SINGULAR KERNELS
33
equation for the Markov semigroup (3.20). 3. More importantly, the mild Itˆo formula does not provide
a semimartingale representation for smooth functionals of λ.
In this section we aim to prove an Itˆo formula for time-dependent, smooth (in an appropriate sense)
functionals F : [0, T] × H1 →R that
a) is well-adapted to the structure of (3.1),
b) results in desirable semimartingale representations for the process F(t, λ(t)), and
c) serves as a cornerstone for the well-posedness proof of our backward Kolmogorov equation,
Theorem 5.1 below.
We achieve this goal by studying the limit of strong Itˆo formulae for H1−valued smooth approximations
of the mild solutions λ (3.4).
A glimpse at the strong Itˆo formula on H (4.4) immediately reveals the following challenge:
In order to obtain an analogous “strong” Itˆo formula on H1, one needs to make sense of H1−directional
derivatives along the singular kernel K. However, as we have explained in Section 1.1 K cannot be an
element of H1. For this reason, we introduce below a concept of “singular” directional derivatives that
appears naturally in the subsequent analysis and plays a key role in the analysis of Section 5.
Definition 4.10 (Singular directional derivatives). Let V be a Banach space and L0(Ω; V) the vector
space of V−valued random variables endowed with the metric topology of convergence in probability.

Remark 4.11. We collect here a few observations on the definition of singular directional differentia-
bility.
i) Our definition of singular directional derivatives as limits of classical directional derivatives
is similar to one given in [VZ19, Definition 3.16 and Theorem 3.17].
To be precise, such
differentials are defined in the last reference as limits of Fr´echet derivatives on a (Banach)
space of continuous functions while D is defined here as a limit of Gateaux derivatives on the
Hilbert space H1.
3This observation can already be verified in the much more general framework of mild Itˆo processes that was intro-
duced in [DPJR19]. Indeed, the derivation of a mild Fokker–Planck equation (equation (71), Section 3.2.2 therein) is a
straightforward consequence of a mild Itˆo formula. However, to the best of our knowledge, there is no “mild backward
Kolmogorov” equation for SPDE mild solutions that a) follows from the mild Itˆo formula or b) holds for “non-smooth”
initial data y /∈Dom(A). The non-invertibility and non-commutativity of the associated composition operators and semi-
groups S (see e.g. display (70) in the same reference) appear to be essential sources of difficulty for the aforementioned
task.

Remark 4.14 (Comparison to Malliavin derivatives). It is perhaps instructive to provide a comparison
between singular directional derivatives and the widely used Malliavin derivatives (see e.g. [Nua06])
for functionals F : H1 →R, on an underlying abstract Wiener space (H1, K, γ) (i.e. γ is a Gaussian
measure on a Hilbert space H1 with Cameron-Martin space K ⊂H1). Malliavin derivatives can be seen
as “restrictions” of classical Gateaux derivatives of F to the smaller set of directions K. In contrast,
singular derivatives DF(h) are rather extensions of Gateaux derivatives of F : H1 →R and are defined
with respect to the larger set of directions h ∈K ⊃H1. Indeed, when h ∈H1, DF(h) reduces to a
classical Gateaux derivative per Remark 4.11.
Next, we introduce an appropriate class of functionals that are classically (respectively singularly)
differentiable in time (respectively space) and satisfy certain growth conditions. This is precisely the
class of functionals for which we state our singular Itˆo formula (Theorem 4.18).

Remark 4.19. Theorem 4.18 is, in a sense, interpolating between the strong and mild Itˆo formulae
from Sections 4.1, 4.2. In particular, it shows that sufficiently smooth functionals of H1−valued mild
solutions λ (3.4) satisfy a “strong”-type Itˆo formula that holds for initial conditions in K1. Of course,
such an “intermediate” Itˆo formula relies crucially on the special structure of (3.1). Thus, an analogue
of Theorem 4.18 is not expected to hold for generic SPDEs that do not enjoy the invariance and
smoothing properties of (3.1).
Furthermore, while the strong Itˆo formula holds when the data y, K ∈H1, this singular version
operates a tradeoff: it allows the relaxation K ∈K and in exchange restricts y ∈K1 (recall that K1 ⊂
H1 ⊂K ).
Remark 4.20. The strategy of mollifying, applying Itˆo formula and then taking the limit does not
apply at the level of the Volterra process X itself. Its infinite quadratic variation appears in the limit.
The lift, even though it is not a semimartingale either, is thus better behaved in that sense.

We conclude with a technical remark on the proof strategy of Theorem 4.18:
Remark 4.21. As we already saw in Example 4.12, singular derivatives at a fixed point y ∈H1
DF(y)(·) are not continuous with respect to directions in K . For this reason, in the above proof, we
first extend the “smooth” Itˆo formula from initial conditions y ∈H2 to y ∈K1 by taking n →∞and
then pass to the “singular” limit δ →0. Taking limits in this order allows us to use joint continuity
properties of standard differentials DF which may not be available in the singular limit.


## Page 41


KOLMOGOROV EQUATIONS FOR STOCHASTIC VOLTERRA PROCESSES WITH SINGULAR KERNELS
41
4.4. Applications of the Itˆo formulae. In this section we present a number of applications of
Theorems 4.8, 4.18. The first two, an Itˆo formula with random coefficients for the SVE X and a mild
Fokker–Planck Equation (FPE) for the law of mild solutions λ (3.4) follow from the mild Itˆo formula.
The third, a singular FPE follows from the mild Itˆo formula. Before we proceed, we make here the
following important remarks:
Remark 4.22 (Comparisons between the mild and singular Itˆo formulae and FPEs).
i) In contrast to the the mild Itˆo formula, the singular one does not lead to an Itˆo formula for X.
Indeed, the latter holds for functionals in the class C1,2
T,K (Definition 4.15). Thus it cannot be
applied to evaluation functionals ev0 : H1 →R which, per Example 4.13, are not singularly
differentiable.
ii) In Corollary 4.26, we show that the singular FPE (4.29) with initial data {δy}y∈K1 is uniquely
solved by the law of the state process λ0,y. This is done by duality and essentially follows from
existence of solutions to the backward PDE 5.1(1). In contrast, due to absence of a “mild”
backward PDE we only show existence of solutions to the mild FPE (4.27).
iii) The mild and singular Itˆo formulae and FPEs apply to different classes of test functions. In
particular the class C1,2
T,K are space-time test functions and satisfy much stronger conditions
compared to (spatial only) test functions in C2
b (H1).
4.4.1. Itˆo formula for Volterra processes. For the first application of this section, we “project” the mild
Itˆo formula (4.7) back to the SVE (1.1) and obtain a finite-dimensional Itˆo formula for functionals of
the non-semimartingale process X. The main result of this subsection is the following:
Corollary 4.23 (Itˆo formula for SVEs). Let T > 0, p ≥2q/(q −2), X0 ∈Lp(Ω; H1), X, λ denote the
unique solutions of (1.1) and (3.1) respectively on [0, T]. Next, let f ∈C1,2([0, T] × Rd; R) such that

s ≤t ∈[0, T], it holds that

Proof. We shall apply Theorem 4.8 with V := R and F : [0, T] × H1 →V given by F(t, x) :=
f(t, ev0(x)). From the lift property 3.15 it follows that F(t, λ(t)) = f(t, Xt). In order to show that F
is Fr´echet differentiable in the spatial variable we omit the time-dependence as it does not affect the
proof. To this end, Lemma 2.9 guarantees that ev0 : H1 →Rd is well-defined and continuous. Since
it is a linear operator, ev0 ∈C2(H1) with directional Fr´echet derivatives along h1, h2 ∈H1 given by
Dev0(h1) = ev0(h1), D2ev0(h1, h2) = 0. For f ∈C2(Rd; R), let F := f ◦ev0. By Fr´echet chain rule we
have F ∈C2(H1; R) and DhF(x) = Df(ev0(x))Dhev0 = Df(x(0))h(0) for x ∈H1, h ∈H1. For the
second derivative, an application of the product rule yields:
D2F(x)(h1, h2) = D2f(ev0(x))

Hence the function F satisfies the required differentiability assumptions. Finally, notice that F inherits
the growth properties of f since the evaluation operator is linear and bounded.
By noticing that
ev0(S(t −r)x) = x(t −r) and ev0(S(t −r)h) = h(t −r), the proof is complete.
□
Several observations on the SVE Itˆo formula are collected in the following remark


## Page 42


42
IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
Remark 4.24.
i) Letting s = 0 in (4.25) we see that the first term on the right-hand side reduces to f(0, λ(0, t)) =
f(0, X0(t)) which is a function of the initial curve of the SVE (1.1).
ii) In general, the Itˆo formula for X is tied to the SPDE solution λ. The presence of this infinite-
dimensional process on the right-hand side of (4.25) compensates for the lack of semimartin-
gality of X. Nevertheless, a smooth functional f(X) of an SVE remains an SVE (albeit with
a random, non-convolutional kernel).
iii) The Itˆo formula (4.25) can also be obtained from the (singular) functional Itˆo formula derived
in [VZ19, Theorem 3.17]. Nevertheless, we emphasise that the assumptions in force here are
weaker than those needed for the functional Itˆo formula. We refer to Section 6.2 for a more
detailed comparison. A similar Itˆo formula for SVEs was obtained in [Hub24] by using the OU
lift (see Section 6.1) in negative Sobolev spaces.
iv) The mild and SVE Itˆo formulae obtained above differ from strong Itˆo formulae due to the
presence of the processes S(t −s)λ(s), λ(s, t −s) that appear respectively as arguments in the
differentials of the chosen test functions. Nevertheless, for fixed t ∈[0, T], these processes are
in fact semimartingales in s ∈[0, t].
Remark 4.25. All results in this section continue to hold for SVEs with (probabilistically) weak
solutions.

5. Kolmogorov backward equation
From this point on, Assumption 2.4(iii) is in place. This section is devoted to the proof of our main
result, Theorem 5.1. Before we proceed, we recall the notation H2 := H2

In particular, and in analogy to our proof of the singular Itˆo formula (4.15), we shall obtain the
backward Kolmogorov equation (5.3) as a limit of classical Kolmogorov equations associated to a
“mollified” process λδ. The latter solves the same equation (3.1) as λ, but with K replaced by the
shifted kernel Kδ := S(δ)K which is smooth away from the origin by Assumption 2.6. Note that λδ is
different from Λδ = S(δ)λ defined in the previous section. For any λ0 ∈H2 and δ > 0, λδ is an H1–mild
solution to the corresponding SPDE and, furthermore, Assumption 2.6 guarantees that λδ(t) ∈H2.
Thus λδ is an analytically strong solution in H1 and, for T > 0, φ ∈C2

proof to Appendix A.4.

5.1. Properties of singular tangent processes. We begin here our study of differentiability of the
random solution flow of (3.1). We emphasise once again that the abuse of notation outlined in (2.4) is
also applied at the level of the tangent processes Dyλy(h1) = ζh, Dyλy(h1, h2) = ζh1,h2. In particular,
we will often plug in the matrix-valued kernel K in the place of h1, h2. Of course, as explained above,
expressions like zetaK, zetaK,K will always be interpreted componentwise in the direction.
Before proceding to our analysis, we make the following important remark on the (functional)
differentiability of the nonlinear terms b, σ.
Remark 5.4 (Fr´echet differentiability of coefficients). The nonlinear operators b0, σ0 : H1 →Rd, Rd×m
are twice Fr´echet differentiable (for example, Db0(λ)(h) = ∇b(ev0(λ))ev0(h) for λ, h ∈H1) since they
only depend on the finite-dimensional projections ev0(λ). Consequently, the same is true for the “singu-
lar” coefficients Kb0, Kσ0 : H1 →L2
w(R+; Rd), L2
w(R+; Rd×m). This is in sharp contrast to Nemytskii
operators B : H1 →H1 of the form B(λ)(x) := b(λ(x)), x ∈R+, which, as is well known, are only
Gateaux differentiable (except for trivial cases; see e.g. [GSS23, Remark 6]). This additional regularity

be taken along any direction in H1. This differs to the setting of SPDEs with Nemytskii operators
where, typically, tangent processes are well-defined under additional smoothness of the direction (see
e.g. [Cer01, Chapter 4.2]).

6. Connections and comparisons between infinite-dimensional SVE lifts
We conclude this work by comparing our setting and results to those found in the context of different
Markovian SVE lifts.


## Page 68


68
IOANNIS GASTERATOS AND ALEXANDRE PANNIER
6.1. Ornstein-Uhlenbeck lift. Throughout this section, L denotes the Laplace transform defined on
a sufficiently large space of functions or measures over R+ and we set d = m = 1 in (1.1). As we have
already mentioned in Section 1, the transport SPDE (3.1) is related to the forward curve lift for SVEs.
In this section, we show that the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) lift, a well-studied process that has often
been employed to restore Markovianity of SVEs such as (1.1), can be recovered by a transformation
of the SPDE (3.1).
Initially introduced by Carmona–Coutin in [CC98] and further developed or employed in stochastic
analysis, stochastic control and mathematical finance settings (often with an emphasis on the special
class of affine SVEs) [CT19, CT20, HS19, Ham24, Hub24, AJMP21], the OU lift is described as follows.

stochastic volatility modelling), b) it unifies Musiela’s classical SPDE theory and study of singular

6.2. Path-dependent PDEs and the work of Viens and Zhang. The theory of SVE lifts, that
the present paper contributes to, mainly benefits from the lift solving a stochastic equation in infinite
dimensions. Its solution has better properties than the original Volterra process (Markov, flow, Feller,
see section 3.2) and it is thus desirable to work exclusively in this new space.
In parallel to this
literature, Viens and Zhang [VZ19] proposed a complementary, more “hands-on” approach. Inspired

expectation E[ϕ(X[0,T ])|Ft] with respect to Ft-measurable random variables, where X solves

Before comparing the necessary assumptions for (6.11) to hold and those set forth in this paper, let
us discuss the (dis)similarities between Θt and λ(t).
The process Θt can be seen as a lift of X in the space C[t, T] (even though it is not presented
as such) since Θt
t = Xt. It is very close in spirit to the lift λ studied in this paper and in fact we
have λ(t, x) = Θt
t+x for all x ∈[0, T −t]. Moreover, the Itˆo formula for Volterra processes derived in
Corollary 4.23 via the mild Itˆo formula can also be obtained from (6.11). This emphasises again that Θt
and λ(t) coincide in a pointwise sense. However, the dynamics (and pathwise properties) of the two
processes differ. This is evident from looking at the t-dependence in (6.9) (only in the integral, while
s is fixed) versus (3.4) (both in the integral and in the kernel). The consequences of this distinction
are fully visible in the associated Itˆo formulae.
As a consequence of the mild formulation (3.4),


## Page 71


KOLMOGOROV EQUATIONS FOR STOCHASTIC VOLTERRA PROCESSES WITH SINGULAR KERNELS
71
our singular Itˆo formula (Theorem 4.18) features the additional term Du(t, λ(t))(∂xλ(t)) compared
to (6.11). Heuristically, this extra term comes from the differentiation inside the integral since ∂x is
the generator of the shift semigroup S.
The assumptions of Corollary 4.23 are however lighter than those necessary for (6.11). In the former,
the differentials are all interpreted in the sense of classical Fr´echet differentiation on Hilbert spaces
and the mild Itˆo formula holds both in the case of singular and non-singular kernels without additional
assumptions on the coefficients.
Our convenient framework is tied to SVEs with convolution-type
kernels while the results in [VZ19] also hold for more general kernels (we recall nevertheless that we do
cover the Mandelbrot-van Ness fractional Brownian motion thanks to its representation with a random
initial curve; see Section 1.5.1). In order to guarantee the convergence (6.12), Viens and Zhang instead
impose regularity conditions on Df, D2f with respect to the L∞-norm of the direction and to some rate
of decay around the singularity [VZ19, Definition 3.16]. To the best of our knowledge, these conditions
have only been verified for a few conditional expectations (6.10), all pertaining to Gaussian Volterra
processes (b = 0, σ = 1) ([VZ19, Theorem 4.1], [BJP23, Proposition 2.22], [Pan23, Lemma 4.8]). All of
these led to the well-posedness of associated backward path-dependent PDEs. The situation where X
follows general Volterra dynamics (6.8) is, at the moment of writing, an open problem.
In contrast, the framework introduced in this paper is tailor-made to include the conditional expec-
tations
u(t, λ(t)) = E

ϕ(XT )|Ft

.
The convenient formulation of the Markovian lift in a Hilbert space paves the way for the singular
Itˆo formula (Theorem 4.18) and the backward PDE (Theorem 5.1). Furthermore, the choice of the
forward curve/Musiela parametrisation avoids the awkward jump at t in the state space (6.13) but so
far does not cover path-dependent payoffs.

A.4. Volterra–Gr¨onwall inequality. We recall that the resolvent r for a kernel k ∈L1([0, T], R+)
is defined as the unique solution to
r(t) −k(t) =
Z t
0
r(t −s)k(s)ds,
for all t ∈[0, T],
or, said more concisely, r −k = r ⋆k. The construction of r, as shown in [Zha10, Lemma 2.1] for
instance, reveals that r ∈L1([0, T], R+). Several forms of the Volterra-Gr¨onwall inequality appeared
in the literature, see e.g. [GLS90, 9.8.2] and [Zha10, Lemma 2.2]. For completeness we provide here a
proof of Lemma 5.3 which is a version tailored to our purposes.
Proof. We may write x = f −g + k ⋆x for some g ≥0. The solution to this equation is given by
x = f −g + r ⋆(f −g) and hence
k ⋆x = x −(f −g) = r ⋆(f −g).
Therefore, x ≤f + r ⋆(f −g) ≤f + r ⋆f, since r ≥0.
□
